=== 2026-07-22 UTC (follow-up: Jordan reciprocal trade agreement) ===
Per user query, investigated a newly announced US-Jordan trade deal for relevance to the forced-labor tracker. On July 21, 2026, Ambassador Greer and Jordan's Minister of Industry, Trade and Supply Yarub Qudah signed the U.S.-Jordan Agreement on Reciprocal Trade. Verified directly against the official White House-published agreement text: Article 2.9(1) commits Jordan to prohibit importation of forced-labor-produced goods within five years of entry into force, and to recognize/presumptively enforce US Section 307 (Tariff Act of 1930) Withhold Release Order determinations. Per Law360 and the White House fact sheet, the US in turn committed to apply the 10% forced-labor tariff tier to Jordan (down from the 12.5% tier assigned in USTR's June 2, 2026 determination) once Section 301 forced-labor duties take effect. This is directly relevant to the tracker: it is a binding bilateral commitment (not yet a domestic import ban) that changes Jordan's expected tariff tier. Updated Jordan's row: column 5 changed from "No" to "Partial (bilateral treaty commitment, 5-year prohibition timeline, no domestic ban enacted yet)"; column 6 populated with the official White House agreement-text source per the sourcing-priority rule (direct government source); column 3/4 updated with the deal details and USTR/White House source links. No other rows changed.

=== 2026-07-23 UTC (correction: South Korea reverted to No) ===
Per user question, reviewed whether South Korea's "Fisheries Forced Labor Prohibition Six Acts" (six bills introduced July 15, 2026) actually constitute progress toward an import ban. On closer inspection, the bills amend Korea's own Salt Industry Promotion Act and Fisheries Act to revoke/bar relicensing of domestic business licenses (e.g. salt farms) found using forced labor -- this is a domestic labor-enforcement/licensing measure targeting forced labor occurring within Korea, not a bill establishing a prohibition on importing forced-labor-made goods from abroad. It does not meet the "Partial" bar under the standing classification policy (which requires progress toward an import ban specifically), so the July 23 07:40 UTC reclassification to Partial was incorrect. Reverted South Korea's row from Partial back to No; column 6 cleared per the mandatory rule. No other rows changed.
Push: succeeded (commit 09227c6 on origin/claude/section-301-labour-tracker-etqasl).

=== 2026-07-24 UTC (TOP PRIORITY: USTR FINAL SECTION 301 DETERMINATION ISSUED) ===
USTR has issued its FINAL Section 301 forced-labor tariff determination. Ambassador Jamieson Greer, at President Trump's direction, announced final action on July 23, 2026, replacing the expiring Section 122 10% global surcharge. Verified directly against the primary sources: the USTR press release ("USTR Takes Action in Forced Labor Section 301 Investigations") and the White House presidential action document. Final tariff tiers:
- 10%: Argentina, Bangladesh, Cambodia, Canada, Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, India, Indonesia, Jordan, Malaysia, Mexico, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, United Kingdom, Trinidad and Tobago.
- 10% (net of MFN): European Union, Taiwan.
- 12.5% (net of MFN): Japan, South Korea, Switzerland.
- 12.5% (all remaining economies): Algeria, Angola, Australia, The Bahamas, Bahrain, Brazil, Chile, China, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Egypt, Guyana, Hong Kong, Iraq, Israel, Kazakhstan, Kuwait, Libya, Morocco, New Zealand, Nicaragua, Nigeria, Norway, Oman, Peru, Philippines, Qatar, Russia, Saudi Arabia, Singapore, South Africa, Thailand, Türkiye, United Arab Emirates, Uruguay, Venezuela, Vietnam.
Product exemptions apply per an annex for each economy (raw materials, disrupted-supply items, domestically unavailable goods, compliance incentives); tariff-rate quotas for Bangladesh, Cambodia, Indonesia, and Malaysia are to be established "as soon as feasible" by September 1, 2026. No explicit effective date is stated in the presidential action itself; secondary reporting (not independently verified against a Federal Register final-rule text, which could not be located as a distinct document from the June 5 proposed-action FRN) ties implementation to on/around July 24-25, 2026, with a vessel-loaded-before/entered-before-July-28 grace window mentioned by one outlet (Supply Chain Dive) but not confirmed in the primary documents.

IMPORTANT: the tariff tier a government received is NOT always consistent with its actual enacted-ban status as tracked in column 5 -- USTR's tier assignment appears to reflect a mix of legal and diplomatic/negotiated factors. Two notable discrepancies found this run:
- Vietnam: has an enacted ban (Decree No. 292/2026/ND-CP, July 22, 2026) but still landed in the 12.5% tier, likely because the decree was issued too close to (or after) USTR's determination cutoff. Per the standing classification policy, Vietnam's column 5 status remains "Yes" (based on the actual enacted decree, independent of which tariff tier USTR assigned).
- Trinidad and Tobago: moved from the original 12.5% proposed tier to the final 10% tier, but no primary Trinidad and Tobago government source (gazette, statute, official statement) could be found confirming any enacted ban, tabled bill, or RTA commitment. Per the standing classification policy, tariff-tier placement alone is not sufficient evidence of a ban, so column 5 is left at "No" pending discovery of the underlying legal basis -- flagged as a priority search item for the next run.

One classification change: Philippines upgraded from No to Partial -- on July 24, 2026 (the day after the final determination), the Philippines' Trade, Labor, and Finance departments signed a Joint Administrative Order establishing an inter-agency committee (DTI/DOLE/DOF/Customs/BOI/PEZA) to investigate and act on forced-labor-import complaints under existing customs-law authority; this is an enacted administrative order but functions as an enforcement/investigation mechanism rather than a standalone blanket import prohibition, so it meets the Partial bar, not Yes.

All other 58 rows: no ban-status classification changes. Every row's summary was updated with a brief note confirming its final Section 301 tariff rate and the USTR/White House source, per this run's top-priority finding. No other new bilateral RTAs/trade deals found. This concludes the "watch for the final determination" tracking objective that has been the standing top-priority item since around July 18, 2026 -- future runs should shift focus to: (1) tracking Trinidad and Tobago's unverified legal basis, (2) any Federal Register final-rule text that supersedes the presidential action with more implementation detail (effective dates, exemption annexes), (3) continued monitoring of RTA-commitment economies (Argentina, Bangladesh, Malaysia, Taiwan, Jordan) for progress toward enacted domestic bans, and (4) the usual watch list (India's DGFT gazette-access issue, Jordan's Annex I tariff-figure verification, Malaysia's "null and void" RTA controversy, Pakistan's unsigned RTA, Colombia/Peru/Bahamas's still-pending bills).
Push: succeeded (commit fc59283 on origin/claude/section-301-labour-tracker-etqasl).

=== 2026-07-24 UTC (follow-up: thorough check of Trinidad and Tobago's ban) ===
Per user request, ran a thorough follow-up investigation into Trinidad and Tobago's forced-labor import-ban status, which the prior entry had left at "No" pending verification despite its 10% final-tariff-tier placement. Located and read the full text of USTR's Federal Register final-action notice (FLIP 301 Investigation Final Action FRN, July 23, 2026) via direct PDF fetch and extraction. Its economy-specific determination section for Trinidad and Tobago (#54) explicitly states the 10% rate is "[b]ased on the findings in the investigation of Trinidad and Tobago, including its adoption of a forced labor import prohibition subsequent to the publication of the June 5, 2026 FRN" -- and a separate passage groups Trinidad and Tobago with Cambodia, Guatemala, Honduras, India, and Sri Lanka as economies that "imposed forced labor import prohibitions" between the June 5 and July 23, 2026 notices, following formal Section 303 government-to-government consultations. This is a specific, explicit factual finding in the primary US document, not just an inference from tariff-tier placement.
Searched exhaustively for the Trinidad-side instrument itself: Ministry of Trade, Investment and Tourism media releases (through July 22), Parliament's bills index and e-Gazette (printery.gov.tt), and the Customs Division's import-prohibitions list -- none show a specific enacted Act, Legal Notice, or Order. The only public Trinidad-side trace is a June 12, 2026 Senate statement by Trade Minister Satyakama Maharaj (reported by TTT News) describing a "proposed measure," explicitly not yet enacted at that time.
Given the specificity and authority of the FRN's finding (informed by direct bilateral Section 303 consultations, not merely public inference), reclassified Trinidad and Tobago from No to Yes, sourced to the FRN itself, with an explicit caveat that the underlying domestic instrument's name/date/citation remains independently unverified and should continue to be sought in future runs. === 2026-07-24 UTC (follow-up: Trinidad and Tobago instrument located and verified) ===
Per a link the user provided to Trinidad and Tobago's e-Gazette, located and verified the actual domestic legal instrument underlying Trinidad and Tobago's forced-labor import ban, resolving the caveat left in the prior entry. Fetched and text-extracted the Finance Act, 2026 (Act No. 16 of 2026) directly from printery.gov.tt: assented 25 June 2026, published in Legal Supplement Part A to the Trinidad and Tobago Gazette, Vol. 65, No. 111, 29 June 2026. Section 24 of the Act amends the Customs Act (Chap. 78:01) section 45(1) -- the Act's list of prohibited/restricted imports -- by inserting new paragraph (w): "all goods produced from forced labour as designated by the Minister with responsibility for trade." This is a genuine enacted, gazetted forced-labor import prohibition (framework-style, with the specific goods list left to future ministerial designation), and its 25 June 2026 assent date fits precisely within the window USTR's Federal Register final-action notice had described ("adoption...subsequent to the publication of the June 5, 2026 FRN"). Updated Trinidad and Tobago's row: column 3 now cites the Finance Act directly with the verbatim clause; column 6 upgraded from USTR's FRN to Trinidad's own e-Gazette (a direct government/legislative source, satisfying the top sourcing-priority tier). Ban status remains Yes, now on fully verified primary-source grounds rather than the FRN's general statement alone. No other rows changed.
Push: succeeded (commit a250f2e on origin/claude/section-301-labour-tracker-etqasl).

Two classification changes:
- Dominican Republic: No -> Yes. President Abinader issued Decreto No. 502-26 on July 23, 2026 (the same day its 12.5% tariff took effect), a directly-sourced government decree empowering customs (DGA) to identify, restrict, and prohibit forced-labor-linked imports.
- Nicaragua: No -> Partial. Ley No. 1280 (enacted, published in La Gaceta April 9, 2026 -- a previously-missed older development) adds Article 16 Bis to its customs self-clearance law, requiring a sworn no-forced-labor declaration on imports with customs authority to investigate/penalize falsification; a declaration-and-investigation regime, not an outright import prohibition, hence Partial rather than Yes.

=== 2026-07-27 UTC (correction: Norway upgraded No -> Partial, previously-missed government decision) ===
Per user follow-up questioning Norway's status, ran a dedicated Norwegian-language search and found a real, previously-missed development that this tracker had twice reported incorrectly. On July 21, 2026 -- two days BEFORE USTR's final determination -- Norway's Ministry of Trade, Industry and Fisheries formally announced a trade ban on products made with forced labour ("Innforer handelsforbud mot produkter fremstilt ved tvangsarbeid"), covering import, export and sale of goods made wholly or partly with forced labour including child labour. Trade Minister Cecilie Myrseth: "Tvangsarbeid er selvfolgelig forbudt i Norge i dag, og gjennom dette regelverket innforer vi ogsa et forbud mot import av varer fremstilt ved tvangsarbeid." Implementation is via incorporation of EU Forced Labour Regulation 2024/3015 into Norwegian law through the EEA Agreement, in force no later than December 14, 2027 (simultaneous with the EU), with the government committing to that date even if formal EEA incorporation runs late. Confirmed against the Norwegian government's own site (regjeringen.no) plus NRK (public broadcaster), NTB (national wire, via FriFagbevegelse), and NordiskPost. Norway reclassified No -> Partial: this is a formal government decision with a binding date, but the implementing Norwegian instrument has not been enacted yet, so it does not meet the Yes bar (directly parallel to Israel's cabinet decision, also Partial).

METHODOLOGY FAILURE WORTH RECORDING: the July 25 batch-4 agent actually SAW a report of this (via a low-quality aggregator, Pravda Norway) and explicitly dismissed it as "not credible," reasoning that it cited no law and that a December 2027 date was "inconsistent with an already-imposed ban." That reasoning was half-right and wholly wrong in conclusion: the item was indeed not evidence of an in-force ban, but the underlying event was real and was sitting on regjeringen.no, NRK and NTB the whole time. The correct response to a low-quality source making a checkable claim is to try to verify it against primary/local-language sources, NOT to discard the claim. This is now the FOURTH instance of the same family of miss (Honduras gazette, Vietnam decree, Trinidad omnibus Finance Act, Norway government decision) -- all found only via local-language or primary-source search. Recommend adding to the standing trigger prompt: when any source, however weak, asserts an economy has acted on forced-labor imports, run a local-language + official-government-site check before dismissing it.
Also notable: Norway's July 21 decision predates USTR's July 23 final determination by two days and still did not earn it the 10% tier -- the third such case alongside Vietnam (decree July 22, 12.5%) and the Dominican Republic (decree July 23, 12.5%).
Push: succeeded (commit 8c8fbca on origin/claude/section-301-labour-tracker-etqasl).

=== 2026-07-27 UTC (Norway: date correction + primary-text status) ===
Corrected Norway's announcement date from July 22 to July 21, 2026: NRK timestamps its report of the Nærings- og fiskeridepartementet press release at 21 July 2026, 19:41, with NTB wiring it 22 July 2026 12:02 (updated 24 July). The decision therefore predates USTR's July 23 final determination by two days, not one. Verbatim ministerial quote retrieved from both NRK and NTB: "Tvangsarbeid er selvfølgelig forbudt i Norge i dag, og gjennom dette regelverket innfører vi også et forbud mot import av varer fremstilt ved tvangsarbeid" (Næringsminister Cecilie Myrseth). NTB adds: "Forbudet gjennomføres ved at Norge innlemmer EUs tvangsarbeidsforordning i norsk rett. Nasjonale myndigheter må undersøke mistanker om tvangsarbeid, og om nødvendig stoppe varer på grensen eller trekke dem fra markedet."
PRIMARY-TEXT CAVEAT: there is as yet NO Norwegian statutory text to cite -- the ban is a government decision to incorporate EU Regulation 2024/3015 via the EEA Agreement, with implementing legislation still being drafted. The operative legal text is therefore the EU regulation itself, which could not be retrieved this run: regjeringen.no returns HTTP 403 to automated fetch (browser-accessible), and EUR-Lex returns an HTTP 202 anti-bot interstitial on all attempted URL forms. The regjeringen.no press-release URL is retained as the column 6 government-direct source (its existence and content are corroborated by NRK, NTB and NordiskPost) but its full text has not been read directly. Retrieving EU Reg. 2024/3015 Article 3 verbatim, and any Norwegian høring/lovforslag once published, are carried forward as follow-up items.
Push: succeeded (commit dc19088 on origin/claude/section-301-labour-tracker-etqasl).

=== 2026-07-30 UTC ===
No ban-status classification changes today (tallies unchanged: 15 Yes / 13 Partial / 32 No). All 60 economies re-researched across 6 parallel batches.

OPEN ITEM RESOLVED -- JORDAN'S 10% RATE NOW PRIMARY-SOURCED. Since July 21 the tracker has carried the caveat that Jordan's 10% tariff was "reported by Law360 but not independently verified against the agreement's Annex I," and four separate attempts to retrieve that Annex failed. Using the Federal Register route opened up yesterday, I read USTR's notice text directly: it states the Trade Representative determined, "including consideration of Jordan's commitment in its ART related to prohibiting the importation of forced labor goods," to "impose 10 percent tariffs on products of Jordan, except as provided in Annex I and Annex II, Parts A, N, and O." The operative fact -- Jordan gets 10%, expressly because of its forced-labor commitment -- is therefore now confirmed from a primary US government document. To be precise about what this does and does not settle: it verifies the US-side determination, not the content of the bilateral agreement's own Annex I, which remains unretrieved but is no longer load-bearing. Arab News independently corroborates the 10% cap, so the figure no longer rests on a single paywalled outlet.
Also verified from the same primary text while I had it open: the tier criteria are stated verbatim as 10% for an economy that "imposes a forced labor import prohibition, has committed to impose and enforce such a prohibition through an Agreement on Reciprocal Trade (ART), or has imposed a partial regime" -- matching this tracker's framework exactly; the TRQ mechanism for Bangladesh, Cambodia, Indonesia and Malaysia is tied to those economies' importation of US cotton and textile inputs; and the notice rests solely on Sections 301(b) and 304(a) of the Trade Act of 1974, with zero references to IEEPA or Section 122.

Critically, that ruling does not disturb this action -- confirmed directly against the FR text, which invokes only Trade Act 1974 authority and never IEEPA. Ratification still stalled, no bill tabled. Stays Partial.
- GUATEMALA -- MINTRAB has said it lacks capacity for extraterritorial investigation and will assemble its company list from international-source information via a 90-business-day interinstitutional process with the Economy and Foreign Affairs ministries. List still unpublished; measure still not in force until ~mid-August. Stays Yes.

=== 2026-08-06 UTC ===
No ban-status classification changes today (tallies unchanged: 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No). All 60 economies re-researched across 6 parallel batches. But two long-standing open items finally closed, and one significant US-side legal development landed.

*** THE TRACKER'S SOLE UNRESOLVED PRIMARY-SOURCE ITEM IS NOW CLOSED: INDIA ***
For many runs the DGFT notification PDF "failed to render / returned corrupted binary," and the row rested entirely on secondary legal trackers. The diagnosis was wrong: the file is NOT corrupt. Fetched with a browser user-agent it returns a valid one-page PDF v1.7 (646KB) that contains only a SCANNED IMAGE with no text layer -- pdftotext extracts exactly one character. So every text-extraction attempt was always going to fail. Rendering the page to an image and reading it visually resolved it, and the primary document is now verified:
- DGFT Notification No. 23/2026-27, New Delhi, dated 13th July 2026, headed "To be published in the Gazette of India Extraordinary Part-II, Section-3, Sub-Section (ii)", issued under Section 3 read with Section 5 of the Foreign Trade (Development & Regulation) Act, 1992 and paras 1.02/2.01 of FTP 2023, signed by Lav Agarwal, Director General of Foreign Trade and Addl. Secretary, File No. K-31013/1/2026-DIR NAFTA-Part(4).
- Para 2.20B verbatim: "The import of goods produced or manufactured, wholly or in part, through the use of forced labour is prohibited. The Central Government may, from time to time, specify, by notification, the goods whose import shall be prohibited under this paragraph... The procedure for conducting an enquiry by the Director General of Foreign Trade into the use of forced labour in the production of such goods shall be as prescribed in the Handbook of Procedures, 2023."
- Para 11.64 verbatim: "'Forced Labour' means all work or service which is exacted from any person under the menace of any penalty and for which the said person has not offered himself voluntarily, as defined under the ILO Forced Labour Convention, 1930 (No. 29)."
CHARACTERISATION CORRECTED: secondary trackers called this an "enabling framework" only. The primary text shows the first sentence of Para 2.20B is a flat, self-executing prohibition; the notification power goes to designating specific goods -- structurally like Trinidad's designation-dependent ban. Classification stays Yes, now on primary authority.
EFFECTIVE DATE stated more carefully: the text says effect "after the expiry of 30 days from the date of publication of this Notification in the Official Gazette." The widely cited ~August 12 date assumes Gazette publication coincided with the July 13 notification date; the document is worded prospectively ("To be published") and the Gazette publication date is not independently confirmed, so the row no longer presents Aug 12 as verified.

TAIWAN -- the instrument flagged last run as "forthcoming" HAS NOW BEEN ISSUED. On July 30, 2026 MOEA and the Ministry of Labor jointly issued the "Operating Guidelines for Restricting the Import of Forced Labor Goods" and a companion "Review Committee Establishment Guidelines" under Article 11 of the Foreign Trade Act: a 12-member committee co-chaired by the two ministers must decide within 30 days of a referral, after which the competent authority publishes a notice restricting or lifting the restriction, with Customs notifying importers to return or destroy listed goods. Column 6 upgraded from the USTR fact sheet to Taiwan's own Ministry of Labor announcement (government-direct). Classification stays Partial -- an enforcement regime under existing statutory authority is not an enacted import prohibition, and no Legislative Yuan bill has been tabled.

US-SIDE LEGAL CHALLENGE TO THE WHOLE ACTION (new, verified, cross-cutting): on August 3, 2026 twenty-five state attorneys general -- co-led by Oregon, Arizona and California, with the governors of Kentucky and Pennsylvania joining -- filed suit in the US Court of International Trade challenging the Section 301 forced-labour tariffs. The complaint argues the tariffs exceed Section 301's statutory limits and violate the APA: that USTR failed to negotiate with the countries concerned as Section 304 requires, imposed across-the-board rather than selective action untied to reversing the investigated conduct, and used forced labour as a pretext to reimpose previously rejected tariff revenue, citing officials' own pre-investigation statements. Reported as the third such challenge. This is a US domestic action, not any tracked economy's measure, so no row changes -- but it bears on the durability of the entire action and is recorded here.

Entry into force (30 business days from July 6 publication) still unconfirmed as of Aug 6. The business-day vs calendar-day ambiguity remains unresolved and is still flagged as secondary reporting.

- CHINA -- a batch reported MOFCOM had moved from words to actual countermeasures, citing a single aggregator. That matters for interpretation: if enacted it would be a DESIGNATION-DEPENDENT framework ban, structurally like Trinidad's Customs Act s.45(1)(w) and India's FTP Para 2.20B, not a self-executing prohibition.

=== 2026-08-12 UTC ===
No ban-status classification changes today (tallies unchanged: 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No). All 60 economies re-researched across 6 parallel batches; all six reported no status changes. No new bilateral RTAs with forced-labour clauses, and no economy joined or left the RTA-commitment watchlist.

GUATEMALA -- the substantive find of the run, and a real sourcing advance. dca.gob.gt, which had returned 403 on every attempt for many consecutive runs, was reachable today. That matters more than an ordinary news hit: the Diario de Centro America IS Guatemala's official gazette, so this is the gazette's own outlet reporting the instrument it published, on the day it published it. It quotes the Acuerdo directly, including the operative prohibition and the listing machinery, and critically: both ministries "deberan publicar el Listado Oficial en su portal electronico institucional, dentro de los 90 dias calendario siguientes a la vigencia del presente acuerdo (que sera 30 dias posteriores a la publicacion en el diario oficial)."
Two consequences. First, it CONFIRMS what this tracker already said: the list window is 90 CALENDAR days and runs from VIGENCIA, not from publication. Second, on the long-contested entry-into-force question, the gazette's outlet glosses vigencia as "30 dias posteriores a la publicacion" WITHOUT the qualifier "habiles" -- on its face a calendar count, which would put entry into force around AUGUST 5 and mean the ban is ALREADY IN FORCE. That moves the weight of evidence toward the calendar reading, aligning with DataExport and AGN and against Agexport and Prensa Libre, which both say "30 dias habiles" (~August 17).
It does NOT settle it, and the row does not claim it does. The quoted clause is the LIST deadline; the vigencia figure sits inside a parenthetical gloss, not a verbatim quotation of the acuerdo's vigencia article, which still has never been retrieved. This row was wrong once before in declaring this question resolved, and that mistake is not being repeated -- the honest position is that the date remains contested, with the balance now tilted toward early August. No dated entry-into-force event is reported either way, and no Listado Oficial has been published.

INDIA -- the inferred effective date fell TODAY and nothing confirms it. It references forced-labour cooperation in general terms but contains no import-prohibition clause, and no final signed bilateral text exists. So there is no treaty-level commitment capable of putting Switzerland on the watchlist even on the Jordan reading -- a firmer basis than "no clause found."

ECUADOR -- implementation gap recorded. The ETITF's operational manual, which the COMEX resolution gave a 90-day window to draft and which the mechanism needs before investigations can begin, has still not been published, and no company list or enforcement action has appeared. The mechanism is formally enacted but operationally dormant, consistent with USTR's weak-enforcement assessment.

THAILAND -- re-confirmed via Thai-language labour reporting that the Labour Ministry's bill remains at the DRAFTING stage and has not been submitted to parliament. Held at No: under the standing policy a measure being drafted is not one formally tabled or published. If it is actually tabled, or if a Royal Gazette proclamation or Watchlist/Blacklist appears, Thailand moves to Partial. This is the most likely near-term mover after South Africa.

ISRAEL -- the finding of the run, verified against the Hebrew source directly. The KNESSET ECONOMIC COMMITTEE has UNANIMOUSLY APPROVED the order banning imports of goods made with forced labour. JDN's Hebrew report reads: "Va'adat HaKalkala shel HaKnesset ishra hayom (hamishi) pe echad tzav histori ha'oser larishona al yevu mutzarim she'yutzru be'avodat kfiya" -- the Knesset Economic Committee approved today (Thursday), unanimously, a historic order prohibiting for the first time the import of products produced with forced labour.
DATE PINNED DOWN HERE, which the research pass expressly could not do: the article says "today (Thursday)" and carries the Hebrew date byline 30 Av; 30 Av 5786 corresponds to THURSDAY, AUGUST 13, 2026, and the weekday and Hebrew date agree, so the approval is dated August 13. The order was initiated by the Ministry of Economy after staff work coordinated by Prof. Avi Simhon of the National Economic Council, and the report is explicit that it was taken under the shadow of the US tariff and is aimed at enabling an approach to Washington to cut the tariff on Israeli exports to 10%.
WHY IT IS STILL PARTIAL: the order is expressly a framework -- "a foundational legal infrastructure" that will now allow concrete products to be added to the prohibited list. Every future addition requires FRESH Economic Committee approval, with objection and appeal mechanisms and preparation periods for importers. No product is banned yet, and no signed ministerial order or Reshumot entry has been reported, so the instrument is not yet issued.
FORWARD-LOOKING NOTE now written into the row: once the order is signed and published in Reshumot, Israel should move to YES even with an empty product schedule -- the same reasoning that treats Trinidad and Tobago's Customs Act s.45(1)(w) and India's FTP Para 2.20B as Yes despite being designation-dependent. NEW OPEN QUESTION, raised from the primary text and deliberately NOT acted on. Reading the companion Presidential Document (FR doc 2026-15274) directly, I found this: "for goods of the European Union, Japan, Korea, Switzerland, or Taiwan, section 301 tariffs that are the net of Most-Favored Nation (MFN) tariffs would be consistent with their respective Agreements on Reciprocal Trade or similar arrangements and would be appropriate to encourage these economies to fulfill commitments regarding forced labor imports." That sentence groups Japan, Korea and Switzerland -- all classified No here -- alongside Taiwan, whose Partial rests precisely on an RTA forced-labour commitment, and the phrase "commitments regarding forced labor imports" could be read as implying such commitments exist for them too. I have NOT reclassified anything on it. The sentence is about how the tariff is computed (netting against MFN) and about encouragement; the tariff outcome for Japan, Korea and Switzerland is the 12.5% top-up rather than the 10% tier given to commitment economies; and no agreement text containing a forced-labour import-prohibition clause has been located for any of the three. It is recorded in all three rows as an explicit open question so a future run can settle it by reading the relevant agreement texts themselves rather than re-deriving the same ambiguity. This is exactly the pattern that put Jordan on the watchlist -- a forced-labour commitment buried in an agreement whose headline says nothing about it -- so it deserves a proper check rather than a shrug.

That supplies the missing reason for a delay this tracker had only been restating: the hold-up is on the US side, not Malaysian legislative reluctance. Since the two-year clock to legislate starts only on ratification, Malaysia is unlikely to move off Partial in the near term for reasons outside its own control.

=== 2026-08-25 02:50 UTC ===
No classification changes today. Tally unchanged: 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No. All 60 economies re-researched across six parallel batches, with local-language searches for every "No"/stale economy. But this was not a quiet run: yesterday's open question is now CLOSED, and a large Canada development landed.

YESTERDAY'S OPEN QUESTION IS SETTLED, AND THE ANSWER IS NO IN ALL THREE CASES. Yesterday I flagged, from the Presidential Document (FR doc 2026-15274), that the phrase "for goods of the European Union, Japan, Korea, Switzerland, or Taiwan... would be appropriate to encourage these economies to fulfill commitments regarding forced labor imports" might imply Japan, Korea and Switzerland hold RTA forced-labour commitments, which would have moved all three from No to Partial. I tasked three batches to settle it by reading the actual instruments rather than re-deriving the ambiguity. Results:
- JAPAN: the only US-Japan instrument in force is the 2025 framework agreement (announced July 22, 2025; implemented by EO 14345 of Sept 4, 2025; FR notices 2025-17389 and 2025-17908). Its terms are a 15% baseline tariff, sectoral carve-outs, purchase commitments, an investment facility and auto-standards recognition -- no forced-labour, Section 307 or import-prohibition clause. No separate 2026 US-Japan ART exists.
- SOUTH KOREA: no KORUS FTA or ART text with a Section 307-style IMPORT-prohibition commitment could be located. The KORUS labour chapter carries general ILO-style commitments to eliminate forced labour within Korea's own territory -- a different thing, which does not qualify under the standing policy. Korean-language sources place Korea among the economies USTR found to have no legal forced-labour import prohibition, citing the Sinan salt-farm case, and USTR's fact sheet listing partners with RTA forced-labour commitments does not name Korea.
- SWITZERLAND: no signed agreement has materialised since the November 2025 non-binding framework, and no instrument on ustr.gov, whitehouse.gov, seco.admin.ch or admin.ch contains a forced-labour import commitment. SECO instead argues Swiss law is "at least equivalent" to an import ban -- the argument for not needing one, the opposite of a commitment to adopt one. A "concluded 20.08.2026" negotiation surfaced in search was checked and belongs to Switzerland-CHINA FTA talks, not the US.
All three stay No. The Presidential Document's phrasing is about tariff netting and encouragement, not existing treaty clauses. Each row now records the negative finding explicitly so the question is not reopened without new evidence. Worth having chased: this is precisely the pattern that put Jordan on the watchlist.

CANADA -- a major development, verified by me directly from the primary source, that changes context but not classification. Canada has SUSPENDED its trade negotiations with the United States. From Prime Minister Carney's own statement of August 21, 2026 (pm.gc.ca, Ottawa), which I retrieved and read in full: "this evening, I have decided to suspend trade negotiations with the U.S. and have directed Canada's negotiators to return to Ottawa," citing "last-minute changes in the U.S. proposed terms" that "were unfair, uneconomic, and called into question the reliability of any deal." It continues: "At midnight tonight, the U.S. intends to impose a 50% tariff on roughly $28 billion of Canadian goods. Canada will match those tariffs dollar for dollar." Two things this does NOT mean, both worth stating because they are easy to conflate. First, this is a SEPARATE tariff track from the Section 301 forced-labour action; Canada's 10% forced-labour tier is unaffected. Second, because no agreement was concluded, no new US-Canada instrument exists, so nothing here creates an RTA forced-labour commitment -- the statement contains no reference to forced labour at all, which I confirmed against the full text. Canada's Yes still rests on the enacted 2020 prohibition. What it does do is remove, for now, any prospect of a negotiated instrument carrying a forced-labour clause, leaving the pending consultation outcome as the only live domestic thread in that row. A batch had also reported Aug 19 "deal" headlines; those were premature framework claims that did not survive Aug 21.

PERU -- the dated target lapsed. Galarreta's "a mas tardar Monday August 24" came and went with no filing: no delegated-powers request has reached Congress, no project number appears in congreso.gob.pe, and Diario El Pueblo of August 24 reports the government now expects to present it merely "esta semana" -- a target that has slipped from a named day to an unnamed week. The caveat that matters still stands: reporting names the request's areas as security and organised crime, judiciary coordination, tax simplification and economic reactivation, with no mention of forced labour, so even a filing would need reading for a forced-labour clause before it moved anything.

UNITED KINGDOM -- an old guard refined rather than overturned, and this is the interesting one. Since early runs this tracker has carried a guard rejecting the claim that the UK government "introduced a bill on June 30, 2026," having traced it to a Business and Trade Committee HEARING. It turns out a June 30 government bill does exist: the Immigration and Asylum Bill (bills.parliament.uk/bills/4254), introduced in the Commons on June 30, 2026 and passed at second reading on July 13 by 264 to 90, carrying real amendments to Modern Slavery Act section 54 transparency-in-supply-chains duties -- broadened scope, mandatory content criteria, and penalties reported at up to GBP 1m or 1% of turnover. The original guard was still right about what it actually addressed: this is NOT a forced-labour import-prohibition bill, TISC provisions being corporate disclosure obligations, so the UK stays Partial. But "no bill was introduced on June 30" was too strong a way to have held it. Both halves are now recorded in the row, because the near-miss between a real June 30 bill and a non-existent June 30 import-ban bill is precisely what caused the original confusion.

PHILIPPINES -- the first legislative vehicle that could eventually carry this row to Yes. Albay Rep. Joey Salceda has filed House Bill No. 10848, "An Act Prohibiting the Trade of Goods Produced Wholly or in Part with Forced Labor and Providing Penalties Therefor," which would create a standalone statutory import prohibition with Bureau of Customs exclusion and forfeiture powers and put the July 24 Joint Administrative Order on a statutory footing. It is filed and numbered but not enacted, so it independently supports Partial rather than changing it.

A LEAD I CHECKED AND DROPPED. A batch reported that New Zealand has a narrow prison-labour import provision at section 96 of the Customs and Excise Act 2018, which would have been worth weighing against the "partial regime" limb that the UK sits on. Before acting I checked the row -- and it already says exactly that: "only a narrow prison-labour import ban under section 96 of the Customs and Excise Act 2018, which provides a pathway for a broader ban that has never been exercised." So this is long-recorded and already weighed in holding New Zealand at No, not a new discovery. I had drafted a flag treating it as an unverified new lead and removed it rather than adding a redundant note to the file. === 2026-08-28 03:05 UTC ===
No classification changes today. Tally unchanged: 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No. All 60 economies re-researched across six parallel batches. But the single item this tracker has been waiting on for two weeks finally happened, and the interesting part is that it still does not move anything.

PERU -- THE DELEGATED-POWERS REQUEST HAS LANDED, AND IT APPEARS NOT TO CARRY FORCED LABOUR. I retrieved La Republica's report of 27 August 2026 (19:40h) directly: "Consejo de Ministros aprueba facultades legislativas y presentaria el pedido este viernes 28 ante el Congreso." The Council of Ministers APPROVED the request on August 27 -- after what the article calls "cinco postergaciones" since the President's July 28 announcement -- and it was to go to Congress on Friday August 28. Batch reporting says Premier Galarreta confirmed it was filed that day; the article I read myself uses the conditional "presentaria," so I am recording the approval as directly verified and the actual filing as secondary-sourced. THE DECISIVE POINT: the article describes the request as empowering the government to legislate for 120 days "en seguridad ciudadana, empleo, desarrollo productivo y temas tributarios," and contains ZERO occurrences of "trabajo forzoso" or "forzoso" anywhere -- which I checked directly. Every other account of the scope (66 requests spanning citizen security, MSMEs, employment and social protection, administrative simplification, productive development, tax and customs, state modernisation) likewise omits forced labour. Mincetur Minister Rogers Valencia's pledge that a forced-labour import prohibition would be folded in therefore remains UNCONFIRMED against the text actually delivered. Peru stays Partial, now resting on two prongs: the unassigned July 2 congressional bill, and a filed delegated-powers request whose forced-labour content is unverified. What would move this row is the request's text or official summary showing a forced-labour clause, or a Decreto Legislativo issued under it containing one.

VIETNAM -- eight days out, and the silence is now the story. Decree 292/2026/ND-CP takes effect September 5. As of today there is still NO ministerial HS-code-level goods list under Article 5(2), NO implementing circular (thong tu), and NO General Department of Customs preparation notice, re-confirmed across multiple independent Vietnamese sources. Negatives re-established, several by direct fetch:
- Colombia: eighth consecutive day of direct registry verification. === 2026-08-30 02:30 UTC ===

Tally unchanged at 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No, but this was NOT a quiet run: two findings this tracker reported in previous runs were WRONG and are corrected today, both verified by me against primary or independent sources. No new or supplemental USTR determination since the July 23, 2026 final action (Federal Register 2026-15181, published July 28); all six batches confirmed this independently.

CORRECTION 1 -- HONDURAS: THE STSS LIST WAS PUBLISHED ON AUGUST 21, AND THIS ROW SPENT NINE RUNS SAYING IT WAS NOT. Acuerdo Ministerial SETRASS-527-2026, "Regulacion para la implementacion del Decreto Ejecutivo PCM-014-2026 y aprobacion de la Lista de Mercancias con Prohibicion de Importacion, Primera Edicion 2026," is item 1 of La Gaceta Edition No. 37,226 of August 21, 2026. I read the full text directly today. Article 32 puts it in force on publication; Article 28 approves an Annex I of six registros; Article 29 defers the prohibition 30 calendar days, so ENFORCEMENT BEGINS SEPTEMBER 20, 2026.

The error was methodological and is worth naming precisely, because it produced confident false statements for nine consecutive days. I read only the gazette INDEX -- edition numbers, dates, item counts -- and searched that index for "forzoso" and "STSS", terms which do not appear in index metadata. I never fetched the contents of Edition 37,226. The "34 items" I kept recording for that edition included the Acuerdo as item 1. Everything built on that absence is withdrawn: the "seven-day gazette freeze" narrative, the claim that the freeze breaking made the negative "materially stronger," and yesterday's conclusion that "the delay is not a publishing outage, it is the list itself not being ready." An index is not the document, and a negative drawn from a summary view is not a negative drawn from the text -- the same lesson this tracker already learned for Honduras and Vietnam in the local-language form.

The substance is genuinely interesting. The Technical Commission found that ILO, UN Special Rapporteur, Walk Free and US DOL reports establish forced labour by country or sector but do not identify a specific producer or supply chain, so listing from them would assume all of a country's goods are tainted and create discrimination contrary to the decree's own Article 6. It therefore built the First Edition exclusively from homologated US CBP Final Determinations (Findings), expressly EXCLUDING Withhold Release Orders as resting only on reasonable suspicion, and DECLINED to adopt the Xinjiang/UFLPA entity list on the ground that it was issued by a non-customs authority and so is not shown to be equivalent under Article 3(e). Article 14 forbids listing by chapter, sector, country or region without a named producer. The six registros are five Chinese producers plus the fishing vessel Da Wang (Yong Feng Fishery Ltd). Honduras stays Yes; what to watch is now enforcement from September 20, not publication.

CORRECTION 2 -- PERU: YESTERDAY'S "EFFECTIVELY CLOSED" WAS WRONG. The delegated-powers package filed August 28 (Oficio No. 245-2026-PR, 66 requests, 120 days) DOES contain a forced-labour import prohibition. I verified this directly in two independent outlets today. Infobae, August 29: "La prohibicion de importar mercancias elaboradas con trabajo forzoso, en linea con compromisos internacionales asumidos por el Peru." Gestion: "medidas para impedir el ingreso al pais de mercancias elaboradas total o parcialmente mediante trabajo forzoso, incluyendo su prohibicion de importacion." It sits in the customs and trade-facilitation bloc, which is why the eight-area summaries -- naming that bloc only as "temas tributarios" -- appeared to omit it. My error was reasoning from an absence in short press summaries to a positive finding of exclusion. Mincetur Minister Valencia's pledge did survive into the delivered text. Peru stays Partial, because delegated powers are a request to legislate rather than a measure, but the basis is now stronger than at any prior point and this is the likeliest route to Yes. Caveats kept: no expediente number located on congreso.gob.pe, and the clause is confirmed from two independent reports of the text, not the filed text itself.

NEW ZEALAND -- election date resolved as NOVEMBER 7, 2026; the October 1 figure this row carried for many runs was wrong. NZ Herald, RNZ, interest.co.nz and ABC News all attribute it to Luxon's own announcement, and beehive.govt.nz carries a release titled "General Election to be held on 7 November." Honest limit: the body text of that official release still will not load, so this rests on convergent secondary reporting citing the announcement, not a primary text I read. The Fair Supply September 24 figure is discarded. The select-committee report-back deadline is TOMORROW, August 31, with no early report-back, extension or import-focused amendment found.

Vietnam: six days from the September 5 effective date of Decree 292/2026/ND-CP, still no implementing circular, no HS-code list and no customs notice -- re-confirmed in Vietnamese. This is now the most time-sensitive open item.

Continuing negatives, all direct: Colombia's MinCIT register fetched for a tenth consecutive day, byte-identical again at 80,541 bytes, ceiling still Decreto 1284, zero "forzoso", fifteen days past the closed window; Israel's claimed order rejected on an eleventh consecutive check; the Bahamas register blocked for a twentieth day; India's DGFT index 403 for a ninth run; South Africa re-verified for a thirty-fourth run and held at No (announced intent to consult is not a tabled measure -- standing offer to upgrade on the looser reading if wanted).

Also logged: Chile hosted USTR deputy Jeffrey Goettman August 24-25 for tariff-exclusion talks (negotiation, not a measure, no classification change). Guards that held: Japan, South Korea and Switzerland not reopened; Nigeria and Oman re-confirmed at 12.5% with the bogus 27.5%/10% figures not repeated; the Indonesian "7 working days" figure did not resurface; a Nicaragua/Dominican "Decreto 502-26" mixup was caught by a batch and discarded.

No classification changes; tally holds at 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No. But this was the run with the most judgment in it so far: TWO reclassifications were proposed by research passes, one clearly wrong and one genuinely arguable, and a near-miss would have manufactured a government position that does not exist.

THAILAND -- upgrade to Partial proposed, taken seriously, REJECTED on the primary text. A pass argued the June 2026 Cabinet decision meets the standing policy's Partial limb (iv), "a formal government decision with instrument pending." That is a fair reading of the secondary summaries, so I went to the Thai government's own PRD release. The Cabinet "มีมติเห็นชอบ" on two things: acknowledging the investigation findings, and approving the ESTABLISHMENT of a committee chaired by the Labour Minister. That committee's job is to set criteria and then PROPOSE a Watchlist/Blacklist/Risk List back TO THE CABINET ("เสนอบัญชีรายชื่อเสี่ยง ... ต่อ ครม."). So the decision creates a body that will later propose measures -- it is not a decision to adopt a measure whose instrument merely awaits publication. No list has been proposed, let alone approved or gazetted, and "ห้ามนำเข้า" (prohibit import) appears ZERO times in either account. That puts Thailand alongside South Africa's intent-to-consult, not alongside a pending instrument. Stays No, with the trigger now named precisely: a Risk List approved by Cabinet, or a Section 5 Royal Gazette notice.

VIETNAM -- downgrade to Partial proposed and rejected, because the policy is explicit. The standing policy says Yes covers an actually enacted instrument EVEN IF NOT YET FULLY EFFECTIVE. An operational gap is not the instrument failing to exist: the decree is issued, published, and in force from today, September 5. The pass proposing the downgrade in fact conceded the decree "satisfies Yes-type criteria as issued," which is the whole point. Vietnam stays Yes. On the substance, the day before it bites: no eleventh-hour circular, no Bo Noi vu HS list, no customs notice. A pass fetched luatvietnam.vn's full article on the 23 prohibited-goods groups -- it lists them by managing ministry and mentions no thong tu and no HS list anywhere -- and I re-fetched customs.gov.vn again today with zero mentions of the decree or "cuong buc". Why the gap matters more today than yesterday: Vietnamese reporting states the operative rule as "viec xac dinh hang cam nhap khau phai can cu ma so HS va mo ta chi tiet hang hoa trong danh muc do bo truong ... cong bo" -- identifying a prohibited good MUST be based on the HS code and description in the ministerial list. If that is right, Vietnam now has a prohibition in force with no operative means of identifying what falls under it. Vietnam stays Yes -- the instrument is enacted, published and in force, exactly what the policy asks -- but this row should stop calling it a pending implementation question and start calling it a live ENFORCEABILITY question.

No classification changes; tally holds at 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No. No new or supplemental USTR determination -- reconfirmed at the Federal Register API, still the same four USTR documents since July 29, none forced-labour related. NEW ZEALAND -- the strongest evidence this row has ever had. Until now everything rested on a single RNZ article reachable only through search snippets. A Bell Gully client note of September 1 sets out the select committee's ACTUAL recommended amendments in detail, and every one is disclosure-regime mechanics: the NZ$100m threshold now measured over two preceding accounting periods; group and joint reporting; balance-date alignment; a ~24-month transition; substitution of an Australian modern-slavery statement for dual reporters; a new false-statement offence up to NZ$200,000; pecuniary penalties up to NZ$600,000; narrowed personal liability. That is a positive account of what the committee actually changed rather than an inference from silence, and it settles the question at this stage: what emerged is a transparency statute with teeth, not an import ban. MY OWN SLIP, FIRST. I launched only FOUR of the six research batches in my first pass -- batches 1 and 4 were omitted -- and did not notice until I went to check on them. I launched both immediately and all six reported before I finalised, so the run is complete; but the two late batches had less time in hand than the others, and that is worth knowing when weighing their depth. Recording it because the alternative is a silently thinner run.

COLOMBIA -- the negative became MUCH stronger, and the change matters. For two weeks this row could only report that the decree register was static at Decreto 1284 of 25 August. So MinCIT has signed and published decrees running some seventy-nine numbers past the previous ceiling, in exactly the days since the comment window closed, and NOT ONE is the forced-labour decree: "forzoso" appears zero times on the register. A ministry demonstrably able to sign decrees and declining to sign this one is a far stronger negative than a quiet register. The draft is still labelled "Proyecto de Decreto" with nothing added since the 3 September document set, twenty-four days past the closed window.

PERU -- hard dates at last. And a concrete intermediate event is scheduled: Premier Luis Galarreta appears before the Comision on SEPTEMBER 9 at 3:00 p.m. to justify the urgency of each of the 66 points -- the first occasion on which the forced-labour item could be discussed on the record. One partial-trimming data point exists and cuts neither way: the Defence Commission, one of thirteen specialised commissions, has already reported approving delegation on migration, security and disaster matters -- a different bloc entirely from the customs/trade-facilitation bloc where the forced-labour prohibition sits. El Peruano was reached by a route that works (diariooficial.elperuano.pe/Normas, after busquedas.elperuano.pe 404'd yesterday) and its September 7 digest returned "No se encontro informacion".

A CORRECTION TO THIS TRACKER'S OWN FRAMING -- UNITED KINGDOM. For several runs I have written that Committee-stage amendments are typically published in the days just before first sitting, and treated the approach of September 10 as "the window that matters". That was wrong about this bill. The September 10 first sitting is an ORAL-EVIDENCE session, not line-by-line consideration, and the Committee was only recently opened to written evidence. Line-by-line scrutiny -- where amendments are actually moved and voted -- comes afterwards, with the Committee reporting by 5:00pm on November 3. So the amendment window is later and wider than I implied, and nothing should be read into the absence of an amendment paper on September 10. Parliamentary primary sources remain blocked for a ninth run.

NEW ZEALAND -- a fresh false-claim candidate, logged not adopted. Note the shape of the error: it revives the very October 1 figure this row already investigated and discarded once -- which is exactly how a discredited date creeps back in. Added to the guard list.

ALGERIA: joradp.dz is NOT blocked -- it redirects to /HAR/Index.htm and its inner frames fetch fine with HTTP 200 -- but they carry only navigation. Architecturally unreadable by this method; no inference either way.

A CORRECTION TO YESTERDAY'S HONDURAS ENTRY. On September 8 I recorded the gazette ceiling as "still Edition No. 37,237 of September 3", implying nothing had been published since. The fix is the tracker's oldest lesson applied to itself: open the edition, do not trust a remembered ceiling.

A RELATED OBSERVATION WORTH KEEPING. The leyes.hn index's ITEM COUNTS DRIFT for editions already published: Edition 37,235 read 97 items on September 5 and reads 89 today; 37,237 read 58 on September 7 and reads 57 today. The index is evidently regenerated rather than static, so item counts must never be used as fingerprints for whether an edition changed -- only contents can settle that. This tracker has cited those counts as if they were stable identifiers, and should stop.

A CLAIM THAT WOULD HAVE UPENDED THE WHOLE FILE, CHECKED AND REJECTED. A search summary asserted that a Supreme Court had "invalidated the tariff". === 2026-09-13 02:50 UTC ===
No classification changes. Tally unchanged for a fifteenth consecutive run: 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No.
Two genuinely new findings, one of them a refutation the tracker has been circling for six weeks.

TOP-PRIORITY CHECK, VERIFIED BY ME DIRECTLY: no new or final USTR Section 301 forced-labour
determination. Newest USTR document is 2026-18775, a 2027 National Trade Estimate comment request on
public inspection ahead of a 14 September date -- NOT a forced-labour action. A full-text Federal Register
search for "forced labor" + "section 301" since 1 August returns COUNT 0. The July 28 notice (2026-15181)
remains the last forced-labour action.

1. JORDAN -- THE "ENTERED INTO FORCE AUGUST 1" CLAIM IS NOW POSITIVELY REFUTED, not merely unconfirmed.
   Six-plus Arabic outlets have carried it for weeks and this row kept declining to adopt it purely for
   lack of official confirmation. The way in came sideways: yesterday's Malaysia finding showed these
   reciprocal-trade agreements share a template -- forced-labour obligation at Article 2.9(1), entry into
   force via exchange of written notifications plus 60 days -- which implied the Jordan text has an
   equivalent. It does. I fetched the agreement on whitehouse.gov: "This Agreement shall enter into force
   60 days after the date on which the Parties have notified each other in writing of the completion of
   their respective applicable internal procedures required for the entry into force of this Agreement."
   THE AGREEMENT WAS SIGNED 21 JULY 2026. Sixty days from signature is ~19 September, and the clock does
   not even start until notifications are exchanged, which nothing shows has happened. Entry into force on
   1 AUGUST -- eleven days after signature -- is ARITHMETICALLY IMPOSSIBLE under the treaty's own terms.
   Rejected on primary-text grounds. A likely source of the error was also found: the OLD US-Jordan FTA
   genuinely "دخلت حيز النفاذ في 17 كانون الأول 2001", so outlets may be transposing that language.
   This matters substantively, not just for tidiness: Article 2.9(1) runs its five-year prohibition
   deadline from entry into force, so a wrong start date misdates the deadline by months. The clock has
   NOT started. Caveat kept: the fetch returned the entry-into-force article as 6.6(2) while my query had
   suggested Malaysia's 7.2, so the NUMBER is held loosely; the 60-day mechanism itself came back
   identically from two independent retrievals.

2. THAILAND -- A NEW PATHWAY, flagged but explicitly not adopted. Thailand-US ART negotiations are
   reported in their final stages (Nation Thailand 8-10 Sept: every major issue settled; Thai Examiner
   10 Sept: an Anutin-Trump call could finalise it). Given the template finding above, a signed Thai
   agreement carrying an Article 2.9-equivalent would put Thailand on the RTA-commitment watchlist and
   move it No -> Partial on treaty grounds with no domestic Thai measure at all. Nothing moves today: no
   signed text, no fact sheet, and this tracker does not classify on a negotiation reported to be nearly
   done. Instruction to the next run: when it is announced, fetch the text, do not read the headline.

3. PERU -- both claims this row was carrying as unverified are now VERIFIED, five days from the deadline.
   La Republica of 11 September confirms 52 diputados have voted against across their commissions and
   names the four outstanding: Economia; Desarrollo Agrario; Defensa del Consumidor; and Modernizacion de
   la Gestion del Estado. Diario El Pueblo of 12 September corroborates the six-commission claim and names
   them: "Justicia se suma a las comisiones de Energia y Minas; Infraestructura, Vivienda y Transporte;
   Produccion; Trabajo; y Ciencia". CAUTION PRESERVED: that list says "Produccion" without the "Comercio
   Exterior y Turismo" suffix -- almost certainly the same body that rejected its report 8-10-1 on
   9 September, but not document-verified as one commission. Neither article names trabajo forzoso, so
   yesterday's inferential link is unchanged -- what is established is the SCALE of rejection, not that
   the clause was singled out. The Comision de Constitucion has NOT issued its dictamen; deadline 18
   September; Defence Minister Belaunde is cited to appear before it on 14 September. Stays Partial.

=== 2026-09-16 02:40 UTC ===
No classification changes. Tally unchanged for an eighteenth consecutive run: 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No.
But the most significant external development since the July action landed, and it validates this file.

USTR PUBLISHED A LIST, AND THIS TRACKER MATCHES IT 12 FOR 12.
On 15 September USTR published "USTR Convenes Over 50 Trading Partners to Provide Training Related to
Imposing and Enforcing Forced Labor Import Prohibitions" -- I fetched it directly. USTR "convened
representatives of over 50 trading partners from around the world to provide training related to imposing
and effectively enforcing forced labor import prohibitions", and NAMED TWELVE ECONOMIES as having adopted
measures: "Cambodia, Canada, Ecuador, the European Union, Guatemala, Honduras, India, Indonesia, Mexico,
Pakistan, Sri Lanka, and Trinidad and Tobago". It announces NO new or final Section 301 determination and
no tariff change -- so the top-priority watch item has still not landed.

EVERY ONE OF THOSE TWELVE IS IN THIS FILE'S "YES" COLUMN. Zero misses. That is the first independent,
government-level check this tracker has ever had against its own central judgment.

THE DIVERGENCE IS THE INTERESTING PART. This file has FIFTEEN Yes economies; USTR names twelve. The three
it omits are the DOMINICAN REPUBLIC, EL SALVADOR and VIETNAM. Two readings, neither established:
  (a) USTR simply has not updated its list; or
  (b) USTR is listing OPERATIONAL regimes, not merely enacted ones.
Reading (b) is what this file's own evidence quietly supports, because those three are precisely the rows
where we have documented the worst implementation gaps -- the DR's customs director saying on the record he
cannot act without pautas tecnicas that the labour minister confirms are still being "evaluated", with no
registry eight weeks on; Vietnam twelve days into an in-force prohibition with no HS-code list, no circular
and no customs notice. And the timing argument cuts AGAINST the lag explanation: Decreto 502-26 issued
23 July and Vietnam's Decree 292 on 22 July, both BEFORE the USTR action itself, so a list compiled later
had every chance to include them.
NOTHING IS RECLASSIFIED. The standing policy classifies on enactment, and a press release is not a
classification instrument. But this is recorded on all three rows so that if USTR later treats them as
lacking a prohibition, we know exactly where the divergence began. Note also the list is clearly UPDATED,
not a restatement of the June tier: it adds Honduras, India, Sri Lanka and Trinidad and Tobago (all
post-June enactments) and drops the RTA-commitment economies and the UK -- which makes the three omissions
harder to wave away.

PERU -- opposing commissions rose to SEVEN (Infobae, 15 Sept): Energia y Minas, Infraestructura, Produccion,
Trabajo, Ciencia, Justicia and Defensa del Consumidor, with only Ambiente and Defensa in favour. Defensa del
Consumidor, one of the four we were watching, voted against by ten votes. Desarrollo Agrario and
Modernizacion still unconfirmed. DISCIPLINE MAINTAINED: none of these articles names the customs/trade bloc
or trabajo forzoso, so what is established is the scale of resistance, not that the clause has been struck.
The Comision de Economia's rescheduled vote was TODAY and its outcome was not retrievable -- too recent to
index -- recorded as a GAP, not a negative. No formal extension of the 18 September deadline reported; the
chair's deferral to ~28 September stands.

HONDURAS -- FOUR DAYS OUT. USTR named Honduras, the first American-side corroboration this row has had,
which sits oddly against the Article 15 classification still being unverifiable. Private-sector guidance has
hardened into practical advice: CCIT offering legal assistance, specialists telling importers to obtain
certificates of origin and sworn social-compliance declarations before Monday. 37,243-37,252 all probed,

1. CORRECTION -- I OVER-READ THE USTR PRESS RELEASE YESTERDAY. I recorded the twelve-economy list and
   leaned toward the reading that USTR might be listing OPERATIONAL regimes rather than merely enacted ones,
   arguing "the timing argument cuts AGAINST the lag explanation". I had not quoted the framing sentence in
   full. It reads: "As a result of these efforts and USTR's Section 301 investigation of this issue, BY JULY
   2026, 12 additional economies had adopted measures that prohibit the importation of goods made by forced
   labor, INCLUDING Cambodia, Canada, Ecuador, the European Union, Guatemala, Honduras, India, Indonesia,
   Mexico, Pakistan, Sri Lanka, and Trinidad and Tobago" -- and the release adds "Since then, dozens more
   countries have expressed interest in adopting similar measures". So: it is explicitly a JULY SNAPSHOT,
   not a current roster; "including" is hedged and the release nowhere claims the list is exhaustive; and
   USTR publishes NO inclusion criteria and NO attendee roster, so there is nothing to test an
   operational-versus-enacted theory against. My claim that a later-compiled list "had every opportunity" to
   include a 23 July decree was simply wrong. The confident framing is WITHDRAWN.
   WHAT SURVIVES is narrower and still real: all twelve named economies sit in this file's Yes column, which
   is genuine external corroboration. And the snapshot explains two of the three omissions well --
   Vietnam's decree was issued 22 July but did not come into force until 5 September, and the Dominican
   decree issued 23 July, one day after the Section 301 action itself. EL SALVADOR IS THE ONE THE SNAPSHOT
   DOES NOT EXPLAIN: Article 27-A has been in force since about March 2026, comfortably inside any "by July"
   cut-off. That is now the sharpest open question from the release, and it is recorded as a question, not
   as evidence of anything.

2. CORRECTION -- THE PERU DEADLINE IS 22 SEPTEMBER, NOT ~28. On 15 September I recorded that the 18
   September deadline had slipped to about 28 September because the chair had deferred debate until after
   the 21-25 September representation week. La Republica of 16 September supplies the rule: "De acuerdo con

   cumplen el lunes de la otra semana." The Comision de Constitucion will sit DURING the representation
   week to meet it. The 28 September figure is withdrawn. The lesson, written into the row: a chair's
   scheduling remark is not a deadline; the reglamento is.
   AND THE FIRST GENUINE COUNTERPOINT: three commissions voted on 16 September and they do NOT all run the
   same way. Economia approved an unfavourable report "9 votos a favor, 5 en contra y 2 abstenciones", but
   Modernizacion de la Gestion del Estado approved FAVOURABLY "10 votos a favor" to "nueve en contra", and
   Desarrollo Agrario likewise "11 votos a favor" to "ocho votos en contra". Both commissions this row had
   listed as outstanding have now reported, and both in favour. The picture is mixed, not uniformly
   negative. None of the three touched the customs/trade bloc or named trabajo forzoso; the only vote that
   ever did -- Produccion, 8-10-1 against on 9 September -- still stands.

=== 2026-09-20 03:05 UTC ===
No classification changes. Tally unchanged for a twenty-second consecutive run: 15 Yes / 14 Partial / 31 No.
No new or final USTR determination -- verified by me directly at the Federal Register API.
Today was Honduras enforcement day, and it produced this tracker's most important methodological finding yet.

1. HONDURAS -- ENFORCEMENT TOOK EFFECT, AND WE HAVE BEEN READING THE WRONG SOURCE FOR A MONTH.
   Enforcement day itself produced no visible events: no customs action, detained shipment, broker account,
   SETRASS/Aduanas statement or same-day dispatch could be found in Spanish anywhere. Most plausibly Sunday
   publication lag; graded a thin-coverage negative, not a finding that nothing happened. No postponement was
   found by any route, so the measure took effect as published.
   THE BREAKTHROUGH came from finally asking whether leyes.hn -- the third-party aggregator this file has
   depended on for a month -- is the right source at all. IT IS NOT. Honduras's gazette is published by the
   Empresa Nacional de Artes Graficas, and enag.gob.hn carries a "Gaceta Digital" section indexed by year and
   month. Its September 2026 index lists editions leyes.hn HAS NEVER SHOWN: 37,243 (10 Sept), 37,244 (11
   Sept), 37,245 (12 Sept), 37,246 (14 Sept) and 37,247 (16 Sept). Every one returned HTTP 404 on leyes.hn on
   multiple days while that mirror sat frozen at 37,242 since 9 September. For at least ten days this row has
   been recording a mirror's gaps as though they were the gazette's. ENAG's own "Fecha de creacion" fields
   show it uploads late too (37,244 and 37,246 on 19 Sept; 37,243 and 37,247 on the 18th), so publication
   genuinely lags -- but the mirror lags further, and silently.
   THE NEW EDITIONS WERE CHECKED, NOT MERELY LISTED, because an index is not the document -- the very rule
   that exists because nine runs produced false Honduras negatives in August. Two carried SETRASS acuerdos,
   from the ministry that issued the prohibited-goods list, which is exactly where a second Annex I or the
   missing Article 15 classification would appear. I downloaded both PDFs and extracted their text.
   Edition 37,245 (4.5 MB) holds Acuerdo Ejecutivo No. SETRASS-521-2026 and has ZERO occurrences of "forzos";
   its only import references are to the fuel-price parity system. Edition 37,244 (11.2 MB) holds Acuerdo No.
   SETRASS-549-2026 and has exactly ONE "forzos" -- "cancelacion por reduccion forzosa de personal", a
   compulsory staff reduction in an employment lawsuit -- while its "prohibido" hits belong to a fencing
   federation's statutes. So NEITHER is a forced-labour measure and the substance is unchanged. Editions
   37,243, 37,246 and 37,247 were not opened and are logged as unchecked.
   STANDING INSTRUCTION CHANGED: use enag.gob.hn/index.php/gaceta-digital as the primary source; treat
   leyes.hn as a convenience mirror that demonstrably omits editions. Note the breakthrough arrived precisely
   BECAUSE the mirror was unavailable -- leyes.hn has now 429'd for three consecutive days. Also logged:
   lagaceta.hn is an unrelated Honduran news outlet, not the gazette; tsc.gob.hn returns 403.

2. PERU -- A SCHEDULED DATE AT LAST, though the deadline stays contested. Canal N reports the Comision de
   Constitucion "programo para el lunes 28 de septiembre el inicio del debate sobre el predictamen" -- the
   first firm calendar entry this row has had. Avendano is also quoted describing the clock as expiring "el
   lunes de la proxima semana", genuinely ambiguous between 21 and 28 September. So a SCHEDULED DEBATE on 28
   September now sits against deadline claims of 21 and 22 September: the scheduling is the firmer fact and
   the deadline the softer one, the reverse of how this row read it three days ago. Both known stale traps
   were re-verified at source (RPP dated 14 June 2018; americatv.com.pe dated 11 June 2024).

3. COSTA RICA -- THE OPEN LEAD IS CLOSED. Expediente 19.323 is an essential-services strike and
   collective-bargaining bill, not a forced-labour measure. The PDF still could not be parsed here, so this
   rests on convergent external characterisation rather than the primary text, and is graded accordingly --
   but the lead is resolved well enough to stop carrying. Worth noting it was found by a pass, could have
   been waved away as noise, and was instead chased to a conclusion.

4. MEXICO -- a search-synthesis claim that "STPS confirmed the launch in September 2026" was caught and
   rejected by the pass itself: it traces to no dated sentence, and the underlying articles (1 September)

THE DAY'S SUBSTANTIVE RESULT -- HONDURAS: THE GAZETTE GAP IS CLOSED AT PRIMARY TEXT.
The three editions left unopened when leyes.hn was rate-limited -- 37,243, 37,246 and 37,247 --

quality was checked before any negative was accepted (14,223 / 7,624 / 42,579 words of four or more
letters). All three contain zero occurrences of forzos, forzad, SETRASS or trabajo forzoso.

import prohibitions without naming or altering the forced-labour one; 37,247 is trademarks and a
consultancy contract. The whole September gazette run has now been read, and it contains no second
Annex I list and no Article 15 customs classification.

slugs (20260901-37235 ... 20260916-37247) match edition number to gazette date cleanly and

THE DAY'S FIRST RESULT IS A CORRECTION OF YESTERDAY'S HEADLINE FINDING. Yesterday this log
asserted a SIX-DAY HONDURAN PUBLICATION GAP spanning the entire enforcement period, on the strength
of an ENAG index that ceilinged at edition 37,247. That was wrong, and the cause is simple: THE
INDEX IS PAGINATED, twelve entries to a page, and every read until now had taken page one only.
Page two carries 37,247 through 37,251. There was no gap; the gazette published on 17, 18, 19 and
21 September, with no edition on the 20th because it was a Sunday.

The route there is worth recording, because I reached the right answer by the wrong road. Page
one's contents CHANGED overnight -- 37,241 appeared and 37,247 vanished -- which I reproduced
across four fetches while the vanished edition still downloaded perfectly at its own URL. I
concluded the index was mutable and lossy, and found 37,248 through 37,251 by probing slugs by
construction. A parallel pass found the actual mechanism, pagination, which explains the same
facts more simply and is correct: nothing was ever deleted, the twelve-item window merely
reshuffled. I have adopted that explanation over my own. This is the third time the
index-is-not-the-document rule has paid out -- after the leyes.hn mirror and the Bahamian acts
register -- and the new failure mode is page one of a paginated list mistaken for the whole list.
A second correction follows from the same error: yesterday's "37,241 numbering skip" was also
wrong; that edition exists.

Substantively nothing changes. All four newly found editions were downloaded as genuine PDFs and
text-extracted, with extraction quality checked before any negative was accepted, and none
contains "forzos", "SETRASS", "PCM-014" or "Anexo I". Edition 37,251 of 21 September -- the first
business day under the prohibition, and the most important edition in this row -- is clean. The

bid schedules.

A STRUCTURAL PROBLEM WITH THIS TRACKER SURFACED ON PERU AND IS WORTH MORE THAN TODAY'S NON-RESULT.
A pass could not establish whether today's Comisión de Constitución session convened and graded

consequence applies to every Western-Hemisphere row: Tegucigalpa is 20:19 the previous evening,
Santo Domingo and Washington 22:19, so a same-day event in the Americas can never be captured by
the run bearing that date, and an absence on the nominal day is a scheduling artefact rather than

=== 2026-09-29 03:05 UTC ===

MATERIAL CHANGE TODAY, THE FIRST IN THIRTY RUNS: COLOMBIA MOVES FROM PARTIAL TO YES.
Tally is now 16 Yes / 13 Partial / 31 No, from 15/14/31.

DECRETO 1444 DEL 28 DE SEPTIEMBRE DE 2026 has been issued, "por el cual se prohíbe la importación
de bienes producidos total o parcialmente mediante trabajo forzoso u obligatorio, y se dictan otras
disposiciones". I verified it twice at primary source. The title, number and date were read on the
Ministry of Commerce's own decree register, whose "Última actualización" line now reads 28 de
septiembre de 2026 -- that register had ceilinged at Decreto 1418 for eleven days. I then downloaded
the decree PDF itself (2,191,640 bytes) and extracted it: 3,038 words, with 31 occurrences of
"forzoso", 21 of "prohib" and 17 of "importaci". Article 1 bars entry into the national customs
territory of goods extracted, produced, manufactured or transformed wholly or partly through forced
or compulsory labour, across every stage of the supply chain; Article 2 defines forced labour by
reference to ILO Convention 29; Article 18 brings it into force the day after Diario Oficial
publication. It is Yes rather than Partial because the policy reserves Yes for an actually enacted
instrument even if not yet fully effective, and this is a signed, numbered, dated decree on the
issuing ministry's own register -- no longer the "Proyecto de Decreto" tracked here since the
comment window closed on 15 August, forty-four days ago. One thing is flagged, not hidden:
publication in the Diario Oficial itself was not confirmed, and since Article 18 keys entry into
force to it, the decree may be enacted but not yet in force. That does not change the
classification under this file's policy, but the date should be pinned next run.

PERU: THE PREDICTAMEN LANDED, AND IT IS NEGATIVE. Yesterday's timezone fix worked exactly as
predicted -- Sunday-evening Lima meant nothing was visible; today's run sees the whole of Monday.

received no formal citación. A PREDICTAMEN now exists recommending "no aprobación y envío al

Proposición Legislativa 00098-2026-2031-CD. Its central objection is constitutional specificity --
the request "reúne 66 ítems distribuidos en ocho literales" while review identifies "más de 100
asuntos distintos". Both predictions this file made, from Fuerza Popular's minority-report
preparation and the 21 September Gaceta Perú TV report, are borne out. But it has NOT been voted:
the commission was convened for two non-ordinary sessions on 29 and 30 September, and a predictamen
is a draft put to a commission, not a decision of it. The delegation is NOT rejected and the vehicle
is not dead. The customs bloc is confirmed still present in the request as filed, but no outlet

question is unresolved at document level. One attributed detail is explicitly NOT adopted: a pass
reported that La República observed the predictamen's first page carries the session date and voting
